12
A recurring concern among attendees was a potential delay to the IPCC’s next assessment reports. The concern was twofold. First, the reports are needed to inform the second global

stocktake in 2028. Second, our discussions suggest there is concern that climate deterioration is happening faster than initially expected, thus delay risks decisions resting on out-of-
date science. Even if there is agreement to produce the reports in time, there is concern that it can’t be fully accurate, as the timeline may limit contributions from scientists in
developing countries, who are struggling to secure the funding and capacity needed to contribute within the shorter timeframe.

IPCC report timelines were a point of contention at COP31 discussions
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) is the UN body for assessing the scientific
evidence on climate change, and its assessment reports provide the scientific basis for climate
negotiations. The IPCC produces reports in 3 areas, namely 1) Physical Science, 2) Impacts,
Adaptation and Vulnerability, and 3) Mitigation, with the last such reports published prior to the

conclusion of the first global stocktake (which measures progress towards goals set out in the
Paris Agreement and informs the next round of national climate plans, known as NDCs) at COP28
in 2023. Coming into COP31, countries were aiming to agree on the timing for these reports
(having failed to reach agreement at previous IPCC meetings), such that they could be signed off at
the IPCC’s next meeting in December.
Europe pushed for faster IPCC reports, while other nations favoured delay

Typically, the IPCC reports have operated on a 5–7 year cycle, but the exact timings require
agreement among IPCC members. At COP31, European diplomats pushed for a trajectory that
would allow the next reports to inform the second global stocktake, which opened at COP31 and is
due to conclude at COP33 in 2028. Some nations favoured a longer timeline, with Kenya arguing
that scientists from developing countries needed more time to contribute, while India and Saudi

Arabia were also said to favour a delay. Some stakeholders at COP31 suggested the reports will
likely paint an alarming picture of the climate, and publishing them before the stocktake would
add pressure for stronger action.
Discussions will reconvene at December’s IPCC meetings
There is concern that agreement will not be reached in time. The longer the impasse continues,
the less time scientists have to prepare for publication. The IPCC Chair has previously been quoted

as stating that agreement must be reached by the end of 2026 if the reports are to be ready to
contribute to the second stocktake. Alongside (and somewhat linked to) disagreement over the
report timelines, nations will need to reach agreement on the IPCC’s budget for the remainder of
the current cycle.
IPCC reports and the global stocktake: key dates
Dec 2023 | COP28, Dubai
First global stocktake concludes; outcomes used to inform next round of
national climate plans

Nov 2026 | COP31, Antalya
Second global stocktake opens; countries again fail to agree a timeline for
the next IPCC reports
Dec 2026 | IPCC meeting
Discussions to reconvene; the IPCC Chair has said agreement is needed by
end-2026 for the reports to be ready in time to contribute to the 2nd
stocktake
Nov 2028 | COP33 (host unresolved)
Second global stocktake scheduled to conclude; outcome will inform the
next round of global climate plans.
Dec 2015 | COP21, Paris

Countries adopt the Paris Agreement. Article 14 establishes global
stocktakes every five years, unless parties agree otherwise.
Nov 2021 | COP26, Glasgow
First global stocktake begins, with information collection and preparation.
Apr 2022 | IPCC AR6 mitigation report released
IPCC publishes the 3rd AR6 working-group report, on mitigation of climate
change. This gave time for the findings of the 3 reports to contribute to the
first stocktake’s outcomes.

TAK EAW AY 6   |   TR AD E
Trade is still a point of contention

13
Cumulative value of EU imports from CBAM sectors by market (2015-24)
Source: BNEF, Deutsche Bank Research.
Note: Chart displays nominal value of cumulative imports to the EU by source markets.
Developing nations, including China, India and the African Group, pushed back against unilateral climate-related trade measures, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism
(CBAM) a particular target. We expect trade to remain a source of friction in climate diplomacy, while cleaner producers should gain a global trade advantage.

Several nations have sought to bring trade into climate discussions at COP
The African Group negotiating bloc submitted a request prior to the event to have an item on
climate-related trade measures formally added to the COP31 agenda. The attempt was not
successful. Meanwhile, both India and China came into COP31 promising to use the event to push
back against climate-related trade mechanisms, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment
Mechanism (CBAM) a particular point of contention. Developed countries continue to defend such

measures, arguing that they are necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases
where foreign competitors face lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic
industries.
At COP30, countries agreed to create a separate space away from COP to discuss climate-
related trade measures, yet this did not stop trade entering discussions
A senior member of China’s delegation described the new platform as “not enough”, calling for

stronger cooperation on measures including defining product carbon footprints and traceability of
agricultural goods to prevent deforestation. They also added that EU-China climate relations had
been damaged by trade tensions. Saudi Arabia and Russia also raised trade during COP31
discussions. A call in the first draft of the cover text “to phase out unilateral climate-related and
trade-restrictive measures” by the end of 2027 had no prospect of success, yet it reflects the desire

of developing countries to continue raising this topic at COP.
In some negotiating tracks, disputes over trade contributed to the collapse of discussions
In a sign of how prevalent trade was in Antalya, an EU official claimed that there was “no room” at
the talks where trade was not raised, describing this as “not helpful”. Indeed, trade was also cited
as the factor behind the collapse of COP31 negotiations on climate change and agriculture, after

some parties demanded that the talks discuss the EU’s CBAM due to claims over its impacts on
fertilisers.

### Page 14

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
There was notable support for electrification goals
The COP Presidency positioned a new “35-by-35” electrification goal as a headline item
for COP31. The goal seeks to raise the share of electricity in global final energy use to
35% by 2035 (vs. c.23% currently) and was generally well received in Antalya, with more
than 80 countries reportedly endorsing it, including China and the EU.
No clean energy requirement drew scrutiny, but was a pragmatic step

As we highlighted in our COP31 preview, the lack of reference to clean energy drew
scrutiny, with concerns that the pledge would fail to deliver climate benefits if fossil fuels
were used to generate the additional electricity. Despite the EU’s eventual endorsement
of “35-by-35”, some member states were reportedly reluctant initially to sign up for this
reason. While we acknowledge these concerns, the pledge could also be viewed as a
well-crafted initiative that addresses energy security while building the infrastructure

needed for significant emissions reductions, even if not immediately. The absence of a
clean energy requirement (though it was implied) was also seen by some as a pragmatic
step that enabled wider participation, as reflected in the high number of nations
pledging support at COP31.
Despite the lack of a clean energy requirement, some countries still opposed the
inclusion of the “35-by-35” goal in the final cover decision
Some developing nations were against its inclusion on the grounds that they viewed it as

potentially restricting development choices. There was also opposition from some of
these countries against the imposition of top-down sectoral targets which do not make
allowances for varying national circumstances. Moreover, while the emphasis of the COP
Presidency on the initiative was likely a key factor behind why it received strong support,
some nations felt that this focus took away from other priorities at the event.

TAK EAW AY 7   |   EL ECTR I F I CATI ON
Electrification was the clearest common ground, with 80+ countries backing the “35-by-35” goal
14
Electrification was the clearest area of common ground in Antalya. The voluntary “35-by-35” pledge, which aims to raise electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035, was
endorsed by more than 80 countries, including China and the EU, and drew strong business support. The pledge does not formally require clean energy, which drew criticism.

However, we viewed the ambiguity as a way to facilitate broader agreement and grow the infrastructure needed for a more meaningful shift away from fossil fuels. The effort also
aligned with broader energy security goals. We expect continued focus on this topic at future COP events.
Business engagement on electrification at COP31 was high
This was evident in the COP31 Electrification Delivery Package, which sought to highlight
billions in investment to support areas including grids and the electrification of key

sectors such as transport and industry. The UN highlighted that more than 260
businesses with more than $4tn in annual revenue “announced or profiled” actions in
support of these objectives at COP31. Meanwhile, the Utilities for Net Zero Alliance
(UNEZA) announced combined 2026 annual investment plans across grids, renewables
and storage of at least $156bn, a 5% increase on 2025 and a 35% rise vs. 2024.

### Page 15

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
AI was a missed opportunity, with just 2% of Blue Zone events focused on it
15
AI was a missed opportunity. While the power demands of data centres were acknowledged as a driver of increased energy demand, discussion of the role AI could play in advancing
climate goals was surprisingly limited. Beyond the formal negotiations, COP is also a major educational and climate technology forum, and we had expected the potential for AI to help

accelerate progress towards net zero to feature far more prominently. The Presidency’s Antalya AI Pledge was a welcome step, but drew support from only seven governments, while
support from major AI companies was also limited.
AI-related discussions weren’t as prevalent as we expected
It is unclear whether this reflects fundamental uncertainty among companies about how to scale
AI sustainably or a lack of understanding of how powerful AI could be in solving climate challenges.

In our view, it is likely a combination of both, making this a missed opportunity for COP to educate
companies and governments. That is not to say there weren’t some great AI panels. At one AI
innovation panel we attended, McKinsey noted that AI sales forecasting can help retailers match
ordering to demand and cut avoidable food waste, reducing emissions (roughly a third of food is
never eaten) and delivering monetary savings.
Only 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events featured a notable focus on AI

As a check against our COP experience, we analysed the focus of more than 4,000 panels and
events listed as being held in the Blue Zone at COP31 (those we could gather from the COP31
programme available on the official website). Our AI driven analysis assessed the focus of each
session and determined a maximum of 3 focus topics. Our analysis showed that AI had the lowest
focus of any of the listed topics, with just 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events having a notable focus on

AI. As expected, climate finance and investment was the most frequently addressed topic.
The COP31 Presidency did put AI on the Action Agenda
Türkiye launched the Antalya AI Pledge on 16 November, recognising AI both as a tool for climate
action and a growing source of environmental pressure. Developed with the ITU, this voluntary
framework asks signatories to select measurable commitments, such as powering AI infrastructure
with low- and zero-emission energy and reporting AI’s environmental footprint. By 19 November,

only seven governments had endorsed it, with little support from major AI players, Alibaba being
the notable exception. This could reflect concern that AI will challenge firms’ near-term emission
commitments, especially for tech companies. Here we would highlight a conversation we had with
a leading Chinese solar panel company. When asked how China would handle sustainability issues
from the expected rapid build-out of data centres, an attendee we spoke to said matter of factly:

“China will make data centres sustainable”.

### Page 16

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 9   |   U NI TED  S TATES
The US federal absence weighs on COP, but the midterms boost state-level momentum
16
The absence of the US federal government under the second Trump administration has had a notable negative impact on negotiations and signaling around financing. The Democrats
continue to show up at COP, this year with a message of optimism following the midterm election results. The America Is All In coalition held several panels at COP highlighting climate

commitments and objectives. Democratic governors showed up to remind COP attendees that roughly three-quarters of the US population now have Democratic governors,
strengthening the case that state-level policy will be a key driver of US clean energy and industrial investment. While we tend to agree, the magnitude of the benefit isn’t entirely clear.
The US once again had no federal presence at COP
Explaining the decision to stay away for a second straight COP, a spokesperson for the federal

administration said it was focused on expanding “affordable, reliable, secure energy sources”. This
also meant the US had no formal negotiating presence, though the climate pivot under the second
Trump administration potentially emboldened nations opposed to faster transition timelines.
US State representation and companies did come to Antalya
At the America Is All In pavilion we attended the US Climate Alliance panel “Undaunted”, featuring
Governors Michelle Lujan Grisham (New Mexico) and Tim Walz (Minnesota). Key messages:

– States retain sufficient regulatory authority, economic influence and private sector partnerships
to keep advancing clean energy, industrial decarbonisation and climate resilience, with member
states of the alliance having reduced net greenhouse gas emissions by nearly a quarter
between 2005 and 2024 while also growing their economies by 37%.
– Clean electricity was presented as an industrial competitiveness issue, with manufacturers
demanding reliable access to clean power when deciding where to locate production.

– Affordability and employment were recurring themes, with both governors stressing that
climate policy must deliver visible economic benefits.
– Methane was a focus. New Mexico argued it has combined rising oil and gas output with
stringent methane standards and super-emitter monitoring, resulting in lower emissions.
Governor Grisham signalled hopes of encouraging Texas, which has just elected Democrat Gina
Hinojosa as governor, to adopt similar rules.

– Governor Walz of Minnesota criticised companies that publicly support climate action but locate
manufacturing in states with weaker labour and environmental policies.
– Consistent with our wider observations (see takeaway 8), AI was notably absent from the
discussion, with data centres mentioned only in passing.

In the US (and abroad), the level of alarm over global warming is inching higher
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

### Page 18

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 1 0   |   NATU R E, CAR BON MAR K ETS  & OCEANS
Continued scientist alarm over nature and ocean health raises the importance of scaling financing mechanisms
18
Nature and the oceans returned to the agenda at COP31, a month after the UN’s biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) found most 2030 targets off track. Scientists warned of record
ocean heat, and with an overshoot of 1.5°C looking ever more likely, the case for scaling up finance is growing stronger. Canada’s pledge of up to $392.5mn to the Tropical Forest

Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30, gave forest finance a lift, while Brazil presented a deforestation roadmap outside the formal talks. In carbon markets, the focus has moved
from writing the Article 6 rules to making them work. Buyers want high-integrity credits, governments want faster payments. In our view, buyer confidence must come first.
Scientists again warned of concerns over the health of oceans and nature
The UN held a dedicated biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) in Armenia last month. It

found that progress towards most of the 2030 targets under the Kunming-Montreal
Biodiversity Framework has been insufficient. Scientists made similar points at COP31.
The WMO’s 2026 “State of the Climate” report, published during the summit, said ocean
heat content in the upper 2,000 metres reached a record in 2025, with preliminary data
pointing to further increases in 2026.
The value of these systems is well understood, but financing to support their

protection remains a challenge
On a global level, the economic case for protecting these assets is clear, with the World
Bank estimating that more than half of global GDP depends on nature and its services,
while studies put the value of the ocean economy at between $3-6tn. Developing
countries state that while they want to protect oceans and nature, this can often be at
odds with national economic development goals. Finance flows for conservation and

protection initiatives remain relatively small compared with projects in areas such as
clean energy, while developing countries cite misalignment between their needs and
financing conditions, with accessibility a further challenge.
Scaling the financial sector’s involvement will be key
Our analysis shows 2026 is already a record year for blue bond issuance, though it
remains a fraction of green bond issuance. The Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance

(ORRAA), which funds coastal resilience, secured new commitments at COP31, with
Canada pledging CAD$14mn over four years and Australia AUD$1mn over two.
Mangrove Breakthrough, which backs mangrove protection (coastal forests that shield
shorelines, store carbon and support fisheries), said its tracked large-scale projects
reached almost US$1bn in cumulative value since 2020, up 32% year on year.
On carbon markets, implementation is now the main focus for Article 6

Carbon markets let countries and companies offset emissions by paying for projects that
cut or remove them elsewhere. Article 6 of the Paris Agreement sets UN-backed rules for
such trading, and most technical details were settled at earlier COPs. COP31 left the
rulebook intact, with a review due in 2028, so attention turned to implementation. On 16
November the UN climate secretariat soft-launched the first version of an international
registry for Article 6.2, the country-to-country track (Article 6.4 is the centralised UN

mechanism). This international version lets countries without their own registry record
and track credit transfers.
Several Article 6 deals were announced
Norway agreed to buy up to 12 million tonnes of emissions reductions from solar
projects run by Indonesia’s state utility PLN through 2038, at a reported US$20–40 a
tonne. It pays only once reductions are verified and delivered, funding projects that
would otherwise not be viable. Separately, Singapore approved its first Article 6.2 project,

in Rwanda, which will distribute 300,000 ceramic water filters so households can purify
water without boiling it over firewood or charcoal. It is expected to avoid about 780,000
tonnes of CO₂e in 2026–30.
For carbon markets, buyer confidence should remain the priority
Fourteen governments signed the Antalya Statement on Mobilising Private Finance
for Climate Action on 12 November, with a playbook of eight policy levers to grow
corporate demand for high-integrity credits. Corporates and investors still stress the

need for robust credits that deliver the promised emissions reductions and limit
greenwashing risk, while some governments want faster payments and less bureaucracy.
Campaigners warn that lobbying to loosen the rules will intensify before the 2028 review.
In our view, buyer confidence in credit trading processes is paramount and should
remain the priority.
Note: Deutsche Bank is a member of the Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance (ORRAA).

### Page 19

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, though the host remains unconfirmed at present. COP32 and COP33 will test how well multilateralism can respond to the setback at COP31. With a

second Global Stocktake concluding in 2028, its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

loss and damage fund for
particularly vulnerable
developing countries. COP28
operationalises it in 2023.
Funding pledges remain far
below estimated needs. This
gap is a continuing test of trust
in climate-finance delivery.
2023–2024
2023 | COP28: Dubai
The first Global Stocktake calls
for a transition away from
fossil fuels in energy systems.

It provides a collective
assessment of progress and
informs national climate plans.
2024 | COP29: Baku
The new finance goal calls for
at least $300bn a year for
developing countries by 2035,
with developed countries
taking the lead. It sits
alongside a broader $1.3tn
annual ambition from all
sources.
Fossil-fuel follow-through
COP29’s text omits the fossil-
fuel transition call.
Strengthening or even simply
reiterating the call remains a
contentious topic for nations

at COP.
2025–2026
2025 | COP30: Belém
Countries call for efforts to at
least triple adaptation finance
by 2035 and agree to develop
a just transition mechanism.
2026 | COP31: Antalya
Negotiations over a cover
decision ultimately collapse
following a dispute over the
inclusion of language on fossil
fuels. Electrification sees
strong support. Developing
countries are disappointed
with finance outcomes.
Voluntary ambition
Failure to agree fossil fuel

phaseout steps at COP30 led
to the launch of the Santa
Marta process, with 57 nations
participating in the first
conference. We expect these
“coalitions of the willing” to
gain greater prominence
following COP31’s outcome.
2027
Jun 2027 | Bonn, Germany
UN mid-year climate talks (7–
17 June) take up the divides
left unresolved at COP31.
COP32 | Ethiopia
Addis Ababa hosts COP32
after Ethiopia secured
unanimous African Group
backing.
Delivery and funding access

We expect a focus on
delivering commitments,
climate finance access and
clarity on the tripling
adaptation pledge.
Multilateral decision making
Pressure for COP reform will
intensify after COP31, with the
consensus model in focus
despite significant barriers to
any change. COP32 will need
to show multilateralism can
still deliver.
2028
COP33 | Asia-Pacific
The Asia-Pacific region will
host COP33, but the host
remains unclear. China has
decided not to bid, while India

withdrew its offer in April
2026.
Second stocktake concludes
The second Global Stocktake
concludes at COP33 in
November 2028. Negotiations
shape the final outcome,
which informs the next round
of national climate plans due
in 2030.
Wider context
A November summit could
coincide with the US elections.
COP reform and the
effectiveness of voluntary
coalitions will remain
important questions.

Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
20
TERM DEFINITION
35-by-35 pledge Türkiye’s voluntary goal to lift electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035; endorsed by 80+ countries.

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework including rules on international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of credits between countries.

Article 9
Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Blue Zone UN-controlled area of the COP venue for formal negotiations, pavilions and side events.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.

Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary global roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.
CBAM Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism: EU levy on imports of carbon- intensive goods such as steel, cement, fertiliser and aluminium.
Coalitions of the willing Like-minded countries acting outside the consensus process, such as the 50+ nation Santa Marta process on fossil fuel roadmaps.
Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.

COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum was COP President and led the Action Agenda.
Final COP text / cover text Headline decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome. Also called the cover decision.
TERM DEFINITION
Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.

Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the process to refine these moved forward at COP31.
Global South Informal term for developing and emerging economies, mainly in Africa, Asia and Latin America.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
IPCC Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change: the UN body that assesses climate science. Its reports feed the Global Stocktake.

Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the
shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.

Negotiating groups Blocs coordinating national positions: G77 & China, EU, African and Arab Groups, LDCs (least developed) and AOSIS (small island states).
Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafted final texts and steered government-to-government talks.
Temperature overshoot Global warming exceeding the 1.5°C Paris limit, temporarily or permanently, which science now suggests is likely.

UAE Consensus COP28 (Dubai, 2023) agreement to “transition away from fossil fuels”, the first such call in a COP text.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

F I R S T  T O  M A R K E T

27/11/2026

Cập nhật chính sách thuế quan 
của Mỹ - Section 301 
Đánh giá tác động đến Việt Nam

Cập nhật tổng quan về Section 301 của USTR - Văn phòng Đại diện Thương mại Mỹ 
— Ngày 25/11/2026, USTR công bố kết luận điều tra về việc áp thuế section 301 đối với 
hành vi công suất và sản xuất dư thừa mang tính cơ cấu trong các ngành công nghiệp 
chế tạo tại 14 nền kinh tế, trong đó có Việt Nam.  
— Đối với Việt Nam, mức thuế section 301 theo đề xuất cập nhật ngày 25/11/2026 đã bao 
gồm mức thuế Section 301 liên quan đến lao động cưỡng bức được công bố trước đó, có 
thể lên tới 35% trong kịch bản bất lợi nhất. Trong khi đó, mức thuế có thể giảm xuống 
20% nếu Việt Nam đạt được thỏa thuận ART đáp ứng các điều kiện của USTR 
— Về thời hạn đàm phán: Các bên sẽ gửi ý kiến và đăng ký điều trần trước 05/01/2027, 
phiên điều trần bắt đầu ngày 25/01/2027, sau đó USTR xem xét, đàm phán và ban hành 
quyết định cuối cùng.

Một số nội dung chính của Cuộc điều tra dư thừa công suất  
— Mức thuế Section 301 được áp dụng cho tất cả các mặt hàng xuất khẩu sang Mỹ, ngoại 
trừ danh mục được miễn trừ theo thông cáo của USTR bao gồm (1) Các mặt hàng thuộc 
nhóm áp thuế theo Section 232 (2) 2.121 mặt hàng theo mã HS 8 số thuộc phụ lục thông 
cáo (3) Tài liệu mang tính thông tin (Ví dụ: sách), hàng hóa quyên góp và hành lý cá nhân 
đi kèm hành khách. Đáng chú ý, nhóm điện tử, bán dẫn và CNTT vẫn nằm trong danh 
mục hàng hóa được miễn thuế, qua đó hạn chế phần nào tác động của chính sách thuế 
mới đến hoạt động xuất khẩu của Việt Nam 
— Việc Việt Nam đạt được mức 20% phụ thuộc nhiều vào khả năng đàm phán Hiệp định 
Thương mại đối ứng với Mỹ trước hạn nộp ý kiến vào ngày 05/01/2027. Cụ thể, tháng 
12 sắp tới, Việt Nam được mời tham dự hội nghị cấp cao G20 tại Miami. Chúng tôi kỳ vọng 
đây sẽ là cơ hội để hai bên tiếp tục thúc đẩy đàm phán và tiến tới ký kết thỏa thuận, trong 
bối cảnh quá trình thương lượng đã ghi nhận nhiều tiến triển và đang tiến gần đến kết 
quả cuối cùng. 
— Đối với kịch bản cơ sở 20%: Chúng tôi đánh giá Việt Nam sẽ kém thuận lợi do (1) mức 
này cao hơn so với mức hiện hành là 12.5%, dù vậy vẫn tương đương mức 20% đầu năm 
nên không gây ra nhiều xáo trộn (2) Hầu hết các nước bị áp thuế đều đã có thỏa thuận 
hiệp định thương mại với Mỹ với mức chỉ ở khoảng 15-19%, thấp hơn mức IEEPA ban đầu 
của họ và thấp hơn mức của Việt Nam. 
— Đối với kịch bản theo USTR đề xuất 35%: Đây là mức thuế cao nhất trong số các mức 
thuế được đề xuất đối với 14 nền kinh tế thuộc diện điều tra. Nếu được áp dụng, hàng 
hóa Việt Nam sẽ phải chịu mức thuế cao hơn đáng kể so với các quốc gia Đông Nam Á 
khác và những đối thủ cạnh tranh như Ấn Độ, Bangladesh, mặc dù vẫn thấp hơn mức 
thuế mà hàng hóa Trung Quốc phải chịu (tùy thuộc vào diễn biến đàm phán thương mại 
Mỹ - Trung). Trong trường hợp đàm phán không đạt được kết quả tích cực, chúng tôi

### Page 2

2

F I R S T  T O  M A R K E T 
không loại trừ khả năng Việt Nam sẽ phải đối mặt với kịch bản này.

Đánh giá ảnh hưởng tới vĩ mô và thị trường chứng khoán Việt Nam  
— Chúng tôi cho rằng khả năng cao mức thuế thực tế Việt Nam phải chịu sẽ là 20%, khi 
mà thông tin về đàm phán thuế quan Việt - Mỹ vẫn đang phát đi tín hiệu tích cực. Đây là 
mức thuế tương đương thời điểm đầu năm, do đó chưa tạo ra thay đổi đáng kể đối với 
triển vọng xuất khẩu sang Mỹ. Tuy nhiên, vị thế cạnh tranh của hàng hóa Việt Nam có 
thể suy giảm phần nào khi phần lớn các đối thủ thương mại được hưởng mức thuế thấp 
hơn khoảng 1 điểm phần trăm so với thời điểm đầu năm. 
— Dù vậy, cáo buộc của USTR về chính sách tiền tệ của Chính phủ Việt Nam, bao gồm cả 
việc VND đang bị định giá thấp, sẽ gây khó khăn cho nỗ lực hạ lãi suất của NHNN. Khả 
năng mua vào dự trữ ngoại hối của NHNN để hạ lãi suất, vốn đã trở nên khó khăn trong 
thời gian qua khi tỷ giá USD/VND hồi phục trở lại, nay càng trở nên khó xảy ra hơn khi 
mà NHNN sẽ phải hết sức thận trọng trong việc điều hành tỷ giá trong giai đoạn đàm 
phán thuế quan tới đây. Chúng tôi cho rằng NHNN sẽ không nâng giá mua quyết liệt để 
hiện thực hóa việc mua vào dự trữ ngoại hối, vì vậy việc mua sẽ chỉ được thực hiện khi tỷ 
giá liên ngân hàng giảm xuống mốc 25,500 USD/VND. Kịch bản này sẽ chỉ xảy ra khi cung 
ngoại tệ gia tăng mạnh vào giai đoạn cuối năm đổ nhờ kiều hối, xuất siêu, giải ngân FDI, 
các thương vụ bán vốn cho đối tác nước ngoài và phát hành trái phiếu quốc tế; kết hợp 
với việc đà tăng của chỉ số DXY chững lại hoặc quay đầu giảm.  
— Tác động thực tế đến TTCK sẽ được quyết định bởi mức thuế cuối cùng áp dụng đối với 
Việt Nam, dự kiến sớm nhất được xác định vào tháng 2/2027. Trong ngắn hạn, tác động 
phần nào đến từ yếu tố tâm lý và việc giảm kỳ vọng vào hiệu quả chính sách hạ lãi suất 
của NHNN. Nhìn chung, chúng tôi đánh giá mức độ ảnh hưởng của sự kiện lần này sẽ 
thấp hơn đáng kể so với tháng 4/2025, khi chính sách thuế quan của Mỹ lần đầu được 
công bố và tạo ra cú sốc lớn đối với thị trường tài chính toàn cầu, do thị trường hiện đã 
có sự chuẩn bị và khả năng thích ứng tốt hơn. Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sau những phiên biến 
động mạnh ban đầu, thị trường sẽ vận động trở lại theo những yếu tố mang tính nền 
tảng cơ bản như biến động mặt bằng lãi suất, hoạt động kinh doanh của các doanh 
nghiệp niêm yết, hoạt động mua/bán ròng của khối ngoại cho đến khi có quyết định 
chính thức về mức thuế áp dụng đối với Việt Nam vào đầu năm 2027.

Document 332: Data centres power a broader non-mining capex surge (2026-11-27)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Data centres power a broader non-mining capex surge (2026-11-27).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-27
Pages: 14
Status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #construction
Data centres power a broader non-mining capex surge
By Pat Bustamante & Luka Belobrajdic
15:43 November 26 2026 Share
Private capex surged in the September quarter, rising 12.2%qtr – the strongest read since
the height of the mining boom in the September quarter of 2011. Machinery and equipment
rocketed to its strongest quarterly result on record, led by a return to form in information,
media and telecommunications, while buildings and structures too provided support. Capex

expectations point to further growth ahead.

### Page 2

Private capex surprised to the upside, rising 12.2%qtr in the September quarter of 2026. Following a
sizeable 1.6ppt upward revision to the June quarter result, annual growth accelerated to 18.6%yr.
Today’s outcome exceeded even our top-of-market expectation for a 10.0%qtr rise and was well ahead
of market consensus of 4.3%qtr.
Today’s rise was primarily driven by strength across machinery and equipment (M&E), which posted its
strongest quarterly increase on record, up 23.1%qtr and 34.3%yr. Buildings and structures (B&S)

increased 1.7%qtr and 4.5%yr, providing a solid base of support to the headline. Looking across
sectors, the non-mining economy is leading the charge, with capex surging and mining investment also
providing some support.
Data centres power the non-mining investment surge
As we have previously highlighted, there is an investment surge underway in the non-mining economy.
The epicentre is the data-centre build-out, but the surge extends well beyond data centres themselves

Investment is rising in energy and renewables needed to power new data centres, while services
industries (including finance and other business services) are investing heavily in equipment to take
advantage of the AI revolution. Construction and rental services investment is also rising strongly,
supporting the broader build-out. Indeed, non-mining capex surged 16%qtr and 24%yr, the strongest
year-ended growth ever, outside the rebound from the early-1990s recession.

And there is more to come. Expectations data suggest non-mining capex could rise by around 20% in
year-average terms in 2026–27, which would be the strongest nominal growth on record, even stronger
than the 15% recorded in 2025-26. Importantly, capex estimate 4 already incorporates one quarter of
actual spending, making it a better guide to where investment is ultimately likely to land.
Some of this strength reflects higher prices for electronic equipment. But even after adjusting for

inflation, non-mining capex is expected to grow by around 17% in year-average terms in 2026–27, the
second strongest rate on record, and that comes on top of an already strong 2025–26 (13.6% in year
average terms).
And while non-mining investment is leading the charge, mining investment is also showing signs of life,
rising 1.4%qtr and 3.8%yr. Expectations suggest the sector will continue investing at around this pace
over the 2026-27 financial year.

### Page 3

What does this mean for the economy?
Despite the high import content of data-centre investment, we estimate that around half of the spending
will ultimately flow through to domestic activity, somewhat higher than the RBA’s estimate of around
one-third. Imported equipment still needs to be transported and installed, while the data centres
themselves require structures to house the equipment and supporting infrastructure. Our estimate is
based on input-output analysis that captures these domestic supply-chain linkages. The investment

surge is therefore providing a meaningful boost to activity and incomes, partly offsetting weakness
elsewhere in the economy.
Over the longer term, a larger and more modern capital stock should lift productive capacity and
productivity. But in the near term, the investment surge is primarily a boost to demand, partly offsetting
weakness elsewhere in the economy.
What does this mean for next week’s National Accounts?
Combined with yesterday’s construction data, today’s numbers point to stronger growth in business

investment and construction activity over the quarter, suggesting some upside to our Q3 estimate
(currently sitting at 0.5%qtr). However, with several important partial indicators still to come, we will
provide our full assessment in tomorrow’s GDP preview.
What about inflationary pressures?

### Page 4

Capex price pressures accelerated further, with the headline deflator rising 1.7%qtr, the strongest result
since June 2023 and up from 0.7%qtr in the June quarter. The M&E deflator rose a sharp 3.1%qtr,
compared with an average decline of 0.1%qtr over the previous three years, lifting six-month
annualised growth to 3.3%. The turnaround likely reflects broader inflationary pressures and a softer
Australian dollar, particularly given the import-intensive nature of the renewed surge in data-centre

equipment investment.
Industry detail
Mining capex continues to support the headline, rising 1.4%qtr, and moving 3.8% higher in year-ended
terms. Machinery and equipment led the increase, up 3.2%qtr, while buildings and structures edged
0.5%qtr higher. Non-mining capex rose 16.0%qtr and remains 24.0% higher over the year. The rise
was driven by machinery and equipment, up 27.1%qtr, while buildings and structures rose 2.4%qtr.
As anticipated, information, media and telecommunications led the capex surge, rising 86.7%qtr.

Leading indicators had pointed to renewed strength, with ‘other commercial’ building approvals
remaining highly elevated through H1 2026 and ADP equipment imports reaching another record high
in the September quarter, rising 83.9%qtr. B&S rose 8.9%qtr, while M&E surged 183.5%qtr, rebounding
sharply from last quarter’s 51.1% decline and highlighting the inherent lumpiness of data-centre
equipment investment.

### Page 5

Looking through these quarterly swings, capex in the industry remains 142.7% higher over the year
and is the second-largest source of investment behind mining. Its growing contribution to aggregate
investment is increasingly reminiscent of the early stages of the mining investment boom, with
movements in the sector continuing to dominate headline capex outcomes.
Importantly, the non-mining investment surge extends beyond data centres. Financial and insurance

capex rose 16.7%qtr, with gains across B&S (+32.6%qtr) and M&E (+14.0%qtr). Electricity, gas, water
and waste services rose 7.0%qtr and 20.8%yr to a new quarterly high, led by B&S (+7.7%qtr), while
M&E was flat. The industry should remain supported by the energy transition and infrastructure
requirements associated with data-centre expansion. Retail trade also reached a new quarterly high
following a 6.1%qtr rise, further underscoring the breadth of strength across non-mining investment.

Capex spending plans
Alongside the capex data, the ABS also publishes businesses spending plans for this financial year,
which allows us to assess how the capex spending is likely to evolve over the coming quarters. Usually,
those plans tend to have a bias, with companies first estimates usually erring on the side of caution.
Over time, business tend to revise their plans higher. We apply a methodology, that takes historical
ratios of plans to actuals alongside business conditions which allows us to derive a more realistic

capex estimate in the year ahead.
Estimate 4 for FY2027 came in at $223.7bn, slightly above our expectation of $221bn. Spending

### Page 6

intentions were 17.1% above the equivalent estimate a year ago and were revised 11.8% higher from
Estimate 3 just three months earlier, underscoring the strength of the investment pipeline. Based on the
historical profile of estimates, this implies nominal capex of around $253bn in FY2027, representing
growth of 20.5%yr.
After a year of relatively subdued outcomes, price pressures are beginning to re-emerge, with the
capex deflator rising 1.7%yr. We expect some further acceleration through the remainder of 2026, with

the recent depreciation of the Australian dollar placing upward pressure on the price of imported capital
goods, particularly given the import-intensive nature of data-centre investment. Combined with nominal
growth of 20.5%yr, this implies real capex growth of around 15.8%yr, which would be the strongest
outcome since the mining investment boom in FY2012.

### Page 1

13/11/2026 1

BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IN FRANCE: DOUBLE-DIGIT GROWTH IN BUSINESS CREATIONS,
LOWER INCREASE IN BANKRUPTCIES, AND STABLE NON-PERFORMING LOANS – PARTIAL UPDATE WITH
MONTHLY DATA AS OF SEPTEMBER 2026
ECO CHARTS | BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IS A NEW PUBLICATION AIMED AT PROVIDING AN OVERVIEW OF THE DYNAMICS
OF BUSINESS CREATIONS AND BANKRUPTCIES IN FRANCE, AS WELL AS THE RISKS ASSOCIATED WITH THE LATTER. WE WILL PUBLISH

REGULAR UPDATES TO THIS PUBLICATION.

### Page 2

Double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans
2ECOCHARTSECONOMIC STUDIES
• Business creations rose by 16% y/y as of Q3 2026, marking an acceleration compared with the growth seen in 2025 (5.3% y/y).
• This increase was driven by sectors associated with new information and communication technologies, where the trend is gaining
momentum.
• Meanwhile, business bankruptcies rose by 1.7% y/y as of Q3 2026, according to the data from the Banque de France (vs. 3.6% y/y in

2025). After a more pronounced rise in the first quarter (8% y/y), this increase has since slowed. This moderation could continue, as,
according to Altares, payment delays have decreased to an average of 12.3 days in the second quarter of 2026 (14.1 days a year
earlier), compared with the European average of 13.1 days.
• The rise in business bankruptcies is most pronounced in the services sector, which has simultaneously seen an increase in business

creations. This trend is clear in the retail sector, with a 26% y/y increase in business creations as of Q3 2026, while business
bankruptcies are up 3% y/y.
• In construction and manufacturing, business bankruptcies have been declining since Q2 2026.
• The level of the average ratio of non-performing loans is moderately high and has remained stable for the past year (4.0% of total
loans and advances in Q2 2026). More cyclical than in other sectors, the ratio of non-performing loans in construction reached its

highest level since 2019, at 9.5%.
• The share of underperforming loans, but not yet non-performing (in Stage 2, according to IFRS 9 terminology), which provides an
indication of the future trend in non-performing loans, is stable, standing at 15.4% q/q, and remains below its all-time high (16.1%).
• Despite a significant rise since 2021, the economic weight of bankruptcies — which allows them to be weighted by a proxy for
company size — remains in September, on average, below its Q1 2025 peak (0.54 compared with 0.61, respectively). The economic

weight of bankrupted small businesses decreased but still stands at a high level (2.03).
For more information, please refer to our publication: Chart of the week | French corporates: peaks in creations put peaks in bankruptcies into
perspective. July 22, 2026 – Economic Research – BNP Paribas

### Page 1

< Real GDP growth forecast at 0.9% in FY 2026, 
1.3% in FY 2027, and 1.1% in FY 2028 > 
1. Real GDP growth in the July–September quarter of 2026 was −0.1% on an annualized basis, 
marking the first contraction in four quarters, mainly due to a decline in private consumption. 
2. Real GDP growth in the October–December quarter of 2026 and the January–March quarter of 
2027 is expected to remain in the low- to mid-0% range. This is due in part to weak private 
consumption, driven mainly by declining real purchasing power as inflation accelerates. 
Consequently, Japan’s economy is likely to remain sluggish in the second half of FY 2026. 
3. In FY 2027, lower inflation following the consumption tax rate cut on food and beverages is 
expected to improve households’ real purchasing power and lead to a recovery in private 
consumption. Meanwhile, growth in private non-residential investment is expected to accelerate as 
lower raw material prices improve corporate profits. 
4. Real GDP growth is forecast at 0.9% in FY 2026, 1.3% in FY 2027, and 1.1% in FY 2028. The 
consumption tax rate cut is estimated to lower real GDP by 0.1% in FY 2026 and raise it by 0.3% 
in FY 2027. 
5. Consumer price inflation (all items excluding fresh food) is expected to accelerate to the 3% range 
toward the end of FY 2026 as rising import prices feed through to a broad range of items, 
including food and daily necessities. However, it is projected to slow to the 1% range in FY 2027 
following the consumption tax rate cut on food and beverages. Overall, consumer price inflation 
(all items excluding fresh food) is forecast at 2.4% in FY 2026, 1.5% in FY 2027, and 2.0% in FY 
2028.

### Page 2

1 AI-related goods comprise the 103 product categories classified as “AI-Enabling-Products” by the WTO. AI-related goods accounted for 
17.1% of total exports in 2025.

### Page 3

AI-related goods have recorded pronounced
increases in both prices and volumes, likely
reflecting tighter supply and demand conditions
for semiconductors and electronic components as
global AI investment expands. Export prices of
AI-related goods rose sharply by 24.3% year-on-
year in the July–September quarter of 2026.
Together with the increase in export volumes,
this resulted in strong 38.0% year-on-year
growth in export value. Expanding AI-related

exports are substantially boosting total export value on both a volume and price basis.
These increases in export prices, led by AI-related goods, are also helping to mitigate the deterioration in the
terms of trade associated with heightened tensions in the Middle East. While higher prices for crude oil and
other commodities worsen the terms of trade through rising import prices, export prices have also increased
substantially. As a result, the deterioration in the terms of trade index—calculated by dividing export prices

by import prices—has been relatively limited.
Looking at past episodes of surging commodity prices, during the global commodity price surge around
2005–2008, import prices for crude oil and other commodities rose sharply while export price increases
failed to keep pace, leading to a deterioration in the terms of trade. From 2020 onward, the terms of trade
also deteriorated significantly due to rising commodity prices during the post-COVID-19 global economic

recovery, the surge in commodity prices following Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in 2022, and yen
depreciation. In both episodes, the cumulative deterioration in the terms of trade was at least 30%. By
comparison, the deterioration over the two quarters from the January–March quarter to the July–September
quarter of 2026 was 6.6%. A major difference from past episodes is that higher export prices, led by AI-
related goods, have partially offset the impact

of rising import prices caused by surging
crude oil prices.
However, import prices are still rising faster
than export prices, and the terms of trade
continue to deteriorate. If crude oil prices
remain elevated, income outflows abroad
could weigh on the domestic economy.
Meanwhile, if export prices continue to rise
as AI-related demand expands, this will help
mitigate further deterioration in the terms of
trade.

### Page 4

The wage hike rate in the 2027 Spring Labor Offensive (Shunto) is expected to slightly exceed
the previous year’s level
Real wages, calculated by deflating nominal wages using consumer prices (all items excluding imputed rent),
have maintained positive growth for nine consecutive months since January 2026. Nominal wage (total cash
earnings) growth accelerated from the low-2% range in 2025 to the 3% range after the start of 2026. In
addition, a substantial slowdown in inflation—partly due to government measures to ease the burden of

energy costs—has contributed to the increase in real wages.
Although real wages have recently begun to
recover, they had remained on a downward trend
for approximately four years from 2022, as
nominal wage growth failed to keep pace with
inflation. Compared with the pre-COVID-19
period in 2019, nominal wages in the July–
September quarter of 2026 were 12.6% higher,
whereas consumer prices (all items excluding
imputed rent) had risen by an even larger 16.8%

over the same period. Thus, despite the recovery
since the start of 2026, real wages remain 3.7%
below pre-COVID-19 levels, meaning nominal wage increases have not yet fully offset the erosion in
purchasing power caused by higher prices.
The environment surrounding the 2027 Spring Labor Offensive shows that labor market conditions remain
tight, with the active job openings-to-applicants ratio above 1 and the unemployment rate in the mid-2%
range. Corporate profits continue to reach new record highs despite the impact of heightened tensions in the

Middle East. Consumer price inflation had remained in the 1% range since the start of 2026 due to
government energy relief measures, but it rose to the 2% range in September for the first time in nine months
and is certain to accelerate.
In its basic framework for the 2027 Spring Labor Offensive announced on October 22, the Japanese Trade
Union Confederation (Rengo) set a wage hike guideline of “around 6%, with a minimum of 5% (including
the equivalent of regular pay increases),” raising its demands from the previous year’s “at least 5%.” It also

set a guideline of “¥20,000 and at least 6%” for unions at small and midsize companies, including an
additional amount to narrow wage disparities. In recent years, employers’ offers in the Spring Labor
Offensive have generally been slightly less than 1 percentage point below union demands. Since 2023, labor
unions’ wage demands have risen sharply due to high prices and labor shortages, and actual wage hike rates
have risen accordingly, keeping the gap between demands and offers relatively stable. Given this pattern, if

Rengo’s affiliated unions raise their wage demands in line with its policy, employers are also likely to raise
their offers, pushing up the overall wage hike rate.

### Page 5

Against this backdrop, the current outlook
forecasts a wage hike rate of 5.10% for the 2027
Spring Labor Offensive, remaining high in the
5% range for the fourth consecutive year and
rising slightly from 5.01% in the previous year
(on the Ministry of Health, Labour and Welfare
basis, the forecast is 5.30% for 2027, compared
with the actual result of 5.18% in 2026). The
strong possibility that consumer price inflation
will accelerate from its current level of around

2% to the 3% range around the beginning of 2027—when wage negotiations get into full swing—should also
support wage increases.
Real wages have continued to grow by around 2% year-on-year, but growth is likely to slow gradually as
consumer price inflation accelerates, temporarily turning negative toward the end of FY 2026. However,
growth is expected to turn positive after the start of FY 2027 as the consumption tax rate cut on food and
beverages slows consumer price inflation. In FY 2028, once the effect of the consumption tax rate cut runs

its course, consumer price inflation will rise to around 2%. However, continued large wage hikes will sustain
nominal wage growth in the 3% range, keeping real wage growth at around 1%.
2. Real GDP growth forecast at 0.9% in FY 2026, 1.3% in FY 2027, and 1.1% in
FY 2028
Low growth expected to continue in the second half of FY 2026
The decline in private consumption—the main cause of negative growth in the July–September quarter of
2026—largely reflected the temporary effects of adverse weather. Even after these effects dissipate, however,

accelerating inflation is likely to hinder a recovery in consumption. Consumer price inflation returned to the
2% range in September 2026 for the first time in nine months, but is likely to rise to the 3% range toward the
end of FY 2026 as the pass-through of higher raw material prices to durable goods and daily necessities
becomes more widespread. Private consumption is therefore expected to remain weak in the second half of
FY 2026 as higher inflation erodes real purchasing power.

At present, growth is expected to turn positive in the October–December quarter of 2026, but with private
consumption struggling to grow, it is projected to reach only 0.6% on an annualized basis. In the January–
March quarter of 2027, private consumption is likely to decline quarter-on-quarter as inflation rises to the 3%
range and consumers hold off on purchases ahead of the consumption tax rate cut on food and beverages.
Real GDP growth is therefore likely to remain close to zero, at 0.2% on an annualized basis. Overall, Japan’s

economy is expected to remain sluggish in the second half of FY 2026, mainly because high prices depress
private consumption.

### Page 6

Private non-residential investment continues to recover on an underlying basis, supported by high corporate
profits, but the pace of recovery is likely to remain modest for the time being due to high construction
material prices and delays in construction progress caused by labor shortages. Although global AI-related
demand will support exports, overall growth is expected to remain modest given the persistent drag from the
situation in the Middle East and subdued shipments to China remaining amid weakness in its domestic

demand. As a result, exports are unlikely to drive economic growth.
In FY 2027, lower inflation following the consumption tax rate cut is expected to improve households’ real
purchasing power and lead to a recovery in private consumption. Meanwhile, growth in private non-
residential investment is expected to accelerate as lower raw material prices improve corporate profits.
Growth in the April–June quarter of 2027 is likely to be strong at 2.8% on an annualized basis, mainly driven

by a surge in private consumption as improved real purchasing power reinforces the rebound from pre-tax-
cut spending restraint. Thereafter, growth is expected to continue in the low-1% range on an annualized
basis—above the potential growth rate—led by domestic private demand such as private consumption and
non-residential investment. In FY 2028, although the effect of the consumption tax rate cut will fade, the
continued recovery in private consumption and ongoing increases in capital investment are likely to sustain

growth of around 1% on an annualized basis.
Real GDP growth is forecast at 0.9% in FY
2026, 1.3% in FY 2027, and 1.1% in FY 2028.
The institute estimates that consumers holding
off on purchases ahead of the consumption tax
rate cut on food and beverages will lower real
GDP in FY 2026 by 0.1%, while the subsequent
rebound and improved real purchasing power
from lower inflation will raise real GDP in FY
2027 by 0.3%.
Households’ real disposable income remains below its pre-COVID-19 level

Sluggish household consumption since the start of FY 2026 partly reflects the reclassification of spending as
government consumption following the introduction of free high school tuition and elementary school meals,
as well as adverse weather, including heavy and prolonged rain. Over a longer horizon, however, weak
growth in real disposable income is the primary cause. Households’ real disposable income (seasonally
adjusted) has recently recovered but remains slightly below its pre-COVID-19 level (the 2019 average).

### Page 8

Private consumption is forecast to grow by 0.0% in FY 2026, 1.4% in FY 2027, and 0.9% in FY 2028 year-
on-year.
Price outlook
The rate of increase in consumer prices (all items excluding fresh food, hereafter core CPI) fell to 1.9% year-
on-year in January 2026, dropping below 2% for the first time in three years and ten months (since March
2022). It subsequently remained in the 1% range before rising to 2.1% year-on-year in September, returning
to the 2% range for the first time in nine months.

The rate of increase in food prices (excluding fresh food) continued to slow after peaking at 8.3% year-on-
year in July 2025, reaching 2.7% in August 2026 before accelerating for the first time in 14 months to 2.9%
in September. According to Teikoku Databank’s “Survey of Price Trends at 195 Major Food Companies”
(released October 30), the number of food and beverage items subject to price increases in 2026 (January–
December) is 20,327, slightly below the 20,580 items recorded in the same period of the previous year. Amid

the surge in raw material prices associated with the deteriorating situation in the Middle East, the number of
items slated for price increases over the next three months has risen year-on-year since June 2026, although
the pace has slowed somewhat. The rate of increase in consumer food prices (excluding fresh food) is
expected to accelerate, though it should remain more moderate than in the summer of 2025, partly because
rice prices are expected to continue declining for the time being.

Meanwhile, energy prices have been restrained by the government’s relief measures, but support for
electricity and gas bills temporarily ended with September 2026 usage (reflected in October consumer
prices). Although support will resume for usage starting in December 2026, electricity and gas bills this
winter are expected to be substantially higher than in the previous year. This is because subsidy amounts will
be slightly lower than last year, and the impact of surging crude oil prices since the spring of 2026 will be

reflected in fuel cost adjustment rates with a lag. Additionally, to curb surging gasoline prices, the
government used subsidies to hold the nationwide average retail price of regular gasoline at approximately
¥170 per liter from March through November 2026, but it has announced plans to gradually reduce subsidies
from December onward and raise the benchmark price by ¥5 each month. Consequently, energy price
inflation is expected to accelerate sharply from −0.6% year-on-year in September 2026, reaching double

digits year-on-year after the start of 2027 and contributing more than 1 percentage point to core CPI
inflation.
This outlook assumes that support for electricity and gas bills will continue from the summer of 2027
onward, with subsidies gradually reduced. For gasoline subsidies, the benchmark price is assumed to be
raised in stages to ¥185 per liter in February 2027 and then held at that level.
Core CPI inflation is expected to continue trending upward during FY 2026 and reach the 3% range toward

the end of FY 2026 as rising import prices, led by crude oil, feed through to a broad range of items, including
energy, food, durable goods, and daily necessities. A mechanical estimate of the impact of the consumption
tax rate cut for food and beverages from April 2027 onward indicates that core CPI inflation will be reduced

Document 392: New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Official result and next steps (2026-11-27)

Source PDF: all report pdf/New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Official result and next steps (2026-11-27).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-27
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• As polls suggested, the election was a nailbiter
that has not yet been resolved. Election night
results show a lead to the centre-right on
paper in a significantly enlarged parliament.
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First
and ACT won 67 seats. The centre-left bloc
of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti Māori won 57
seats. There was a swing away from National
to its coalition partners while the centre left
grouping picked up a few seats.

• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling
and will enter parliament with at least 7 seats,
reducing what could have been a larger swing
to the centre-left grouping.
• The size of parliament will expand by around 9
seats as National held onto a large share of its
electorate votes, entitling it to more seats than
implied by its party vote share. As a result,
a bloc would require 66 seats to command a
majority in parliament.
• Several senior National ministers have not

been re-elected, including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. She will continue in a caretaker
role but will need to be replaced in any new
centre-right government.
• Significant uncertainty about the result exists.
National might lose perhaps two marginal
electorates in the final analysis. There are
400,000 special votes to count which may
shift the list seat allocations as these tend
to reduce the share of the centre-right bloc
by 1.6-1.7ppts. Hence, when all is said and

done, the centre-right’s 10 seat advantage on
election night could reduce by 1-4 seats.
• Should this play out the centre-right would not
have sufficient seats to reach a majority in an
enlarged house and would require the support
of the Opportunity Party to govern.
• The final election outcome will not be
known until 27 November. Serious coalition
negotiations likely won’t begin until then
and delays could occur. Should recounts be
required, some further delay is possible.

• Based on these results, we see a higher
probability of a centre-right government
ultimately emerging that likely will require
the support of the Opportunity Party in some
form. The Opportunity Party likely won’t enter
Cabinet but may offer support on confidence
and supply in exchange for policy concessions.
• But a centre-left government can’t be ruled
out if special votes heavily favour the centre
left bloc, and especially if an agreement
between the Opportunity Party and the centre-

right parties can’t be reached.
• Regardless of the final count, the results
suggest a government with weak support and
ongoing vulnerabilities. Hence the prospect of
controversial large changes may be modest.
For a centre-right government that might
limit the extent of fiscal consolidation, while
the centre left’s plans for larger scale tax and
spending initiatives could be trimmed back.
• A centre-right government won’t change the
RBNZ mandate while a centre-left government

likely still will. A new finance minister will be
required with the possibility of ACT leader
David Seymour taking on that role.

New Zealand 2026 General Election:
Preliminary results and next steps.
9 Nov 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz
Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
The result.
The preliminary results of the 2026 General Election,
based on the election night count, are set out in the table
above. In summary:
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First and ACT
won 67 seats.
• The centre-left bloc of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti
Māori won 57 seats.
• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling and will
enter parliament with 7 seats.
These results indicate a swing away from National to its

coalition partners while the centre-left grouping picked
up a few seats. The big gainer was the Opportunity Party
who perhaps took a big share of what otherwise could
have been a larger swing to the centre-left grouping.
The size of parliament will expand by around nine seats,
as National held onto a large share of its electorate
votes and hence has more seats than it would have been
entitled to based on its party vote share. As a result, a
governing bloc would require 66 seats to command a

majority in parliament.
Several of the electorate seats are very tight (see Table
2), so election night majorities could easily be overturned
once special votes are counted. Of particular relevance
is the seat of Wairarapa, where the National candidate is
leading the Labour candidate by just 139 votes. If Labour
were to win that seat (specials favoured Labour in 2020
but National in 2023), all else equal the centre-right seat
count would decline to 66 seats in what would become

a 130-seat Parliament – just enough to command a
majority. But if either NZ First or ACT were to lose a single
party vote seat to the left bloc once specials are counted
then that majority would disappear.
On that score, historically the election night results have
tended to overstate the support of the centre-right bloc
and understate the support for parties on the left of
the political spectrum. For example, over the previous
three elections (2017, 2020, 2023), the current centre-

right bloc’s vote share has declined 1.9%, 1.7% and 1.6%
respectively following the counting of special votes, with
the National Party losing 2 seats on each occasion once
the official results were declared (Labour, the Greens and
Te Pāti Māori have benefited). On this occasion, given its
overhang, a lower party vote share would not lower the
National Party’s final vote count, although it could alter
the size of its overhang and thus the number of seats

required to command a majority. In recent elections both
NZ First and ACT have seen their vote shares decline
following the counting of special votes.
The process from here.
Official results.
The official results will be declared on 27 November,
adding the results of an estimated 400,000 special votes
(with around a quarter of these cast overseas) to the
2.41m votes counted on election night.
We expect discussions between potential coalition
partners will begin immediately to start sketching out the

parameters of a coalition agreement. But given the tight
margins of the current vote count, no firm positions will
be agreed until the results are declared.
The writ and recounts.
After 27 November, the writ is scheduled to be returned
on 3 December – formally certifying who has been
elected. However, that date is subject to any recounts

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
that might be requested. Parliament is required to meet
no later than 14 January (six weeks after the return of the
writ), providing an opportunity for a new government
to test that it has the confidence of the House. It is
worth noting that Parliament can be summoned and
begin functioning even if coalition negotiations are still
occurring. If no coalition or confidence-and-supply
arrangement has been reached by then, negotiations can

simply continue and the incumbent Government remains
in office under the caretaker convention (Ministers in
the outgoing government retain executive authority
temporarily, even if they are no longer MPs).
Given that the Pre-Election Economic and Fiscal
Update was released in late September there is no legal
requirement for the Treasury to publish a Half Year
Economic and Fiscal Update (HYEFU) this year. And given
that it will be some weeks before a government is formed,

it seems unlikely that there will be a government in place
that would request that a HYEFU be produced despite
there being no statutory requirement (certainly there
would be little time to incorporate policy changes that
fall out of new coalition agreements, thus diminishing
its value). Once a government is formed, it will have an
opportunity to lay out its immediate priorities in the
Budget Policy Statement (BPS), which by law needs to
be released before the end of March next year. The new

government could decide to release an interim fiscal
update with the BPS if that was thought necessary.
Potential coalition outcomes.
We see three main likely outcomes:
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First.
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First with the support of the Opportunity Party
on confidence and supply.
• Some coalition of Labour, the Greens, Opportunity and
Te Pāti Māori, potentially with Opportunity and Te Pāti

Māori sitting outside the cabinet.
Our preview of the election discussed the policies of
the main political parties, while the figure on page 5
highlights some points of commonality with the centre-
right and centre-left blocs.
National, ACT and NZ First share a broadly pro-
development and deregulatory agenda, including support
for greater resource development, fewer regulatory
barriers and compulsory or expanded KiwiSaver. Beyond
that common core, National and ACT align particularly

closely on lower taxes and deregulation, while National
and NZ First share an emphasis on infrastructure, regional
development and law and order. ACT and NZ First favour
tighter immigration settings and tighter welfare policies.
The differences are clearest at the edges: ACT advocates
deeper tax cuts, smaller government and more market-
led provision, while NZ First places greater emphasis
on economic sovereignty, regional development and
targeted support for working New Zealanders. National

occupies more of the middle ground, combining fiscal
discipline and a pro-business agenda with increased
infrastructure and housing supply.
The Opportunity Party presents itself as a middle
alternative that can work with the left or the right
groupings. They have five bottom line policies they say
they need to support any new government. These include
competition reform through enhanced Commerce
Commission powers, more restrictions on political
lobbyists and donors, a ten-year plan of investment in the

healthcare sector, an end to bottom trawling in inshore
areas and a phase out elsewhere and for the Government
to recommit to New Zealand’s Paris Agreement
obligations. Their success in meeting these bottom
lines with a new government and their flexibility around
these requirements will be key to their ability to form an
agreement with either side. There is better congruence
of the Opportunity Party’s policies with the left than the
right – so progress here will be critical to watch should

their support be required to form a new government. We
suspect they will be very motivated to avoid the need for
a new election via a hung parliament should push come
to shove.

### Page 4

4
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Labour, the Greens and Opportunity share support
for increased public and social investment, a broader
tax base, greater infrastructure investment, stronger
competition policy, cheaper public transport and a
more active role for government. Labour and the Greens
overlap particularly on higher taxation, public investment
and stronger climate and environmental policy, while
Labour and Opportunity share an emphasis on social

investment and higher public-service wages. The Greens
and Opportunity both favour broader taxation and
stronger climate action, although their preferred tax
instruments differ. Their distinctive priorities are also
clear: Labour places more weight on worker protections
and restoring the RBNZ’s dual mandate; the Greens on
environmental protection, progressive taxation and Te
Tiriti; and Opportunity on land-value taxation, housing
infrastructure, productivity, innovation and evidence-

based policy.

and differences.
